========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, yes, it is nice to meet you too. Thank you for seeing me. I, um, well, I guess the main reason I am here, and it took me a little while to actually make the appointment to be honest, because I kept thinking oh it is just a phase, I am just tired or whatever. But what finally made me actually ask for help is that I just keep forgetting all these little things. And I mean things I normally handle with absolutely no problem at all. 

I work as a secretary, so my entire day is basically about managing details and keeping things running smoothly, you know? But lately, it is like my brain just drops things completely. I will forget to return calls, which is so embarrassing when someone has to follow up with me because that is literally my job. Or, for example, I had this registration form recently that I just needed to fill out and turn in, and it just sat there. I kept meaning to do it and then completely forgetting about it the second I walked away or got distracted by something else. 

And it is happening on my commute, too. I will tell myself all day that I need to remember to stop for an errand on the way home, like picking something up at the store or dropping something off, and then I just drive straight home on autopilot and do not even realize I forgot until I am walking through my front door. It is just getting so frustrating because I am usually the one who keeps everything organized, so having all these little slip-ups is really starting to bother me and I figured I should finally talk to someone about it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Worrying? Um... no, I don't think so. I mean, not about a lot of different things, anyway. Like, obviously I get frustrated with myself about the things I just mentioned, the dropping the ball at work and forgetting my errands, because that's just so unlike me and it's super annoying. But I wouldn't call it worrying, exactly? 

Like, my mom is a huge worrier—she will literally lose sleep over whether she left the coffee maker on or if the neighbor's dog is eating enough, just random stuff all the time. But I've never really been like that. I'm usually the one telling everyone else to just take a breath and calm down. I don't sit around stressing about finances or health or, I don't know, what people think of me. If I forget to return a client's call, I don't agonize over whether they're secretly mad at me or if I'm going to get fired, I just think, 'Ugh, seriously, again?' and I fix it and apologize. 

So it's more just... irritation with my own brain right now, rather than feeling anxious or worried about life in general. I just like things to be handled, and when they aren't, I get annoyed at myself, but I definitely don't spend my day worrying about everything under the sun. I've always been pretty even-keeled that way, which is why this whole forgetting phase is so baffling to me, because I don't even have the excuse of being stressed out about a bunch of other things, you know?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, see, that's the thing. I've been thinking about this a lot lately, trying to figure out if this is really a new thing or if it's always been there, just hiding, you know? And when I really look back, I mean really look back to when I was little, like elementary school age... yeah, I guess I kind of was that kid. 

I was always the one who forgot to bring home the permission slip for the field trip until the absolute last second, or I'd bring it home and leave it on the kitchen counter and forget to bring it back to school. My mom used to get so exasperated with me. And my backpack... oh my gosh, my backpack was like a black hole. I would do my homework, I swear I would do it, but then it would just disappear into the abyss of crumpled papers and old snack wrappers at the bottom of my bag, and I wouldn't be able to find it when the teacher asked for it. 

And projects! Don't even get me started on projects. The science fairs, the book reports... I could never just sit down and do them. I always needed my mom or my teacher to basically hold my hand and walk me through it step by step. Like, "Okay, first we need to get the poster board. Now we need to outline the title." If they didn't break it down for me and constantly remind me what the next step was, I'd just wander off and start playing with the dog or reading a book that had nothing to do with the assignment. 

So I guess, yeah, I've always been a little scattered. But I always thought I grew out of it! I mean, I made it through high school, got my job, and I've been doing fine for years. I developed all these little systems to keep myself on track, like color-coded folders and sticky notes everywhere. But now, it's like my systems are failing me, or I'm failing the systems, and I'm right back to being that little girl who can't remember where she put her math worksheet. It's just so bizarre to feel like I'm regressing, you know? Like, why now? Why is it suddenly becoming an issue again when I've managed it for so long?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Interrupt people? Um, no, not really. I mean, I don't think I do that. It's actually kind of funny you ask that, because my husband is totally the one who does that in our house. He gets so excited about a story or an idea, and he just jumps right in before you've even finished your sentence, and I'm always having to tell him, "Hey, let me finish, please!" So I'm pretty hyper-aware of it when other people do it, which probably makes me less likely to do it myself, you know? 

I'm usually more the listener type anyway. Especially at work. As a secretary, a huge part of my job is just letting people talk, taking down the information, and making sure I get all the details right. If I was constantly interrupting my boss or the clients, I'd probably miss something important, or they'd get annoyed with me. So I've really trained myself to just sit back, nod, take notes, and wait for the natural pause in the conversation before I chime in. 

And even outside of work, like with my friends, I'm usually the one who just kind of absorbs what everyone else is saying. Sometimes I actually have the opposite problem, where I wait too long to say something, and by the time I finally have a thought put together and I'm ready to speak, the conversation has already moved on to something else entirely! So then I just keep it to myself because it would be weird to bring up a topic from five minutes ago. But no, blurting things out or talking over people... that's definitely not one of my struggles right now. The forgetting things, yes, absolutely, but I'm pretty good at waiting my turn to talk.

I've been thinking about this a lot lately, trying to figure out if this is really a new thing or if it's always been there, just hiding, you know? And when I really look back, I mean really look back to when I was little, like elementary school age... yeah, I guess I kind of was that kid. \n\nI was always the one who forgot to bring home the permission slip for the field trip until the absolute last second, or I'd bring it home and leave it on the kitchen counter and forget to bring it back to school. My mom used to get so exasperated with me. And my backpack... oh my gosh, my backpack was like a black hole. I would do my homework, I swear I would do it, but then it would just disappear into the abyss of crumpled papers and old snack wrappers at the bottom of my bag, and I wouldn't be able to find it when the teacher asked for it. \n\nAnd projects! Don't even get me started on projects. The science fairs, the book reports... I could never just sit down and do them. I always needed my mom or my teacher to basically hold my hand and walk me through it step by step. Like, \"Okay, first we need to get the poster board. Now we need to outline the title.\" If they didn't break it down for me and constantly remind me what the next step was, I'd just wander off and start playing with the dog or reading a book that had nothing to do with the assignment. \n\nSo I guess, yeah, I've always been a little scattered. But I always thought I grew out of it! I mean, I made it through high school, got my job, and I've been doing fine for years. I developed all these little systems to keep myself on track, like color-coded folders and sticky notes everywhere. But now, it's like my systems are failing me, or I'm failing the systems, and I'm right back to being that little girl who can't remember where she put her math worksheet. It's just so bizarre to feel like I'm regressing, you know? Like, why now? Why is it suddenly becoming an issue again when I've managed it for so long?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt people or blurt things out before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Interrupt people? Um, no, not really. I mean, I don't think I do that. It's actually kind of funny you ask that, because my husband is totally the one who does that in our house. He gets so excited about a story or an idea, and he just jumps right in before you've even finished your sentence, and I'm always having to tell him, \"Hey, let me finish, please!\" So I'm pretty hyper-aware of it when other people do it, which probably makes me less likely to do it myself, you know? \n\nI'm usually more the listener type anyway. Especially at work. As a secretary, a huge part of my job is just letting people talk, taking down the information, and making sure I get all the details right. If I was constantly interrupting my boss or the clients, I'd probably miss something important, or they'd get annoyed with me. So I've really trained myself to just sit back, nod, take notes, and wait for the natural pause in the conversation before I chime in. \n\nAnd even outside of work, like with my friends, I'm usually the one who just kind of absorbs what everyone else is saying. Sometimes I actually have the opposite problem, where I wait too long to say something, and by the time I finally have a thought put together and I'm ready to speak, the conversation has already moved on to something else entirely! So then I just keep it to myself because it would be weird to bring up a topic from five minutes ago. But no, blurting things out or talking over people... that's definitely not one of my struggles right now. The forgetting things, yes, absolutely, but I'm pretty good at waiting my turn to talk.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Um... no, I don't think so. I mean, not about a lot of different things, anyway. But I wouldn't call it worrying, exactly? \n\nLike, my mom is a huge worrier—she will literally lose sleep over whether she left the coffee maker on or if the neighbor's dog is eating enough, just random stuff all the time. But I've never really been like that. I'm usually the one telling everyone else to just take a breath and calm down. I don't sit around stressing about finances or health or, I don't know, what people think of me. If I forget to return a client's call, I don't agonize over whether they're secretly mad at me or if I'm going to get fired, I just think, 'Ugh, seriously, again?' and I fix it and apologize. \n\nSo it's more just... irritation with my own brain right now, rather than feeling anxious or worried about life in general. I just like things to be handled, and when they aren't, I get annoyed at myself, but I definitely don't spend my day worrying about everything under the sun. I've always been pretty even-keeled that way, which is why this whole forgetting phase is so baffling to me, because I don't even have the excuse of being stressed out about a bunch of other things, you know?\n\nDoctor: When you think back to childhood or your school years, were you someone who often forgot things or got easily distracted?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing. My mom used to get so exasperated with me. And my backpack... oh my gosh, my backpack was like a black hole. I would do my homework, I swear I would do it, but then it would just disappear into the abyss of crumpled papers and old snack wrappers at the bottom of my bag, and I wouldn't be able to find it when the teacher asked for it. \n\nAnd projects! Don't even get me started on projects. The science fairs, the book reports... I could never just sit down and do them. I always needed my mom or my teacher to basically hold my hand and walk me through it step by step. Like, \"Okay, first we need to get the poster board. Now we need to outline the title.\" If they didn't break it down for me and constantly remind me what the next step was, I'd just wander off and start playing with the dog or reading a book that had nothing to do with the assignment. \n\nSo I guess, yeah, I've always been a little scattered. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
When did it start getting worse... well, it's actually pretty easy to pinpoint, now that I think about it. It was about 15 months ago. 

That was when I decided to take on this evening certificate program. I've been a secretary for a long time, and I really like my job, but I thought, you know, maybe if I get this business administration certificate, I could eventually move up to an office manager position. It seemed like such a great idea at the time. My husband was super supportive, he was like, "You'd be great at that, you're so organized at work!" Which is ironic now, right? 

But almost as soon as I started those classes, everything just started slipping. Because suddenly, I'm working my full shift at the office—which, like I said, I have my whole routine for—but then I have to rush straight over to the community college on Tuesdays and Thursdays, and then I have coursework on the weekends. It's just like my brain hit a wall. 

The sticky notes and the color-coded folders I mentioned earlier? They just aren't enough anymore. I'm trying to juggle the office managers' schedules, my own homework, reading assignments, and just regular life stuff like grocery shopping and laundry, and that's when I really noticed that my attention and my organization were just... tanking. It's been noticeably harder to manage everything since I started that program. I just feel like I'm constantly dropping the ball now, leaving things behind or forgetting what I was supposed to be doing, whereas before I had it all perfectly balanced. So, yeah, it's been about a year and three months of just feeling completely overwhelmed by it all.

Now we need to outline the title.\" If they didn't break it down for me and constantly remind me what the next step was, I'd just wander off and start playing with the dog or reading a book that had nothing to do with the assignment. \n\nSo I guess, yeah, I've always been a little scattered. But I always thought I grew out of it! I mean, I made it through high school, got my job, and I've been doing fine for years. I developed all these little systems to keep myself on track, like color-coded folders and sticky notes everywhere. But now, it's like my systems are failing me, or I'm failing the systems, and I'm right back to being that little girl who can't remember where she put her math worksheet. It's just so bizarre to feel like I'm regressing, you know? Like, why now? Why is it suddenly becoming an issue again when I've managed it for so long?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt people or blurt things out before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Interrupt people? Um, no, not really. I mean, I don't think I do that. It's actually kind of funny you ask that, because my husband is totally the one who does that in our house. He gets so excited about a story or an idea, and he just jumps right in before you've even finished your sentence, and I'm always having to tell him, \"Hey, let me finish, please!\" So I'm pretty hyper-aware of it when other people do it, which probably makes me less likely to do it myself, you know? \n\nI'm usually more the listener type anyway. Especially at work. As a secretary, a huge part of my job is just letting people talk, taking down the information, and making sure I get all the details right. If I was constantly interrupting my boss or the clients, I'd probably miss something important, or they'd get annoyed with me. So I've really trained myself to just sit back, nod, take notes, and wait for the natural pause in the conversation before I chime in. \n\nAnd even outside of work, like with my friends, I'm usually the one who just kind of absorbs what everyone else is saying. Sometimes I actually have the opposite problem, where I wait too long to say something, and by the time I finally have a thought put together and I'm ready to speak, the conversation has already moved on to something else entirely! So then I just keep it to myself because it would be weird to bring up a topic from five minutes ago. But no, blurting things out or talking over people... that's definitely not one of my struggles right now. The forgetting things, yes, absolutely, but I'm pretty good at waiting my turn to talk.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on tasks or conversations, even when you're trying to pay attention?\n\nPatient: Trouble staying focused... um, no, I wouldn't say that. Especially not with conversations, like I was just saying about how I'm the listener of the group. When someone is talking to me, I'm fully there. \n\nTake my sister, for example. She calls me almost every Sunday, without fail, usually right after she gets back from church, and she will just talk for an hour straight about absolutely everything under the sun. It's her kids, the PTA drama, what her husband is doing with the garage renovation—he's been trying to put in these custom cabinets for like six months now and it's a whole ordeal. But anyway, I listen to every single word. I'm not zoning out or secretly making my grocery list in my head while she talks. I'm actually the one who remembers the details from last week and asks, you know, how the neighbor's dog is doing or if her youngest passed his spelling test. So I definitely pay attention to people. I've never been one to just drift off when someone is looking right at me.\n\nAnd with tasks, it's honestly kind of the same thing. Once I'm actually doing the task, I'm completely locked in. At the office, I have to do a lot of data entry and calendar management. Juggling the schedules for the different managers is a lot, but when I sit down to sort out a scheduling conflict, I am focused. I have this whole little routine where I get my cup of tea—usually peppermint, I try to avoid too much caffeine in the afternoon because it makes me a little jittery and messes with my sleep—and I just power through it. I don't get distracted by the hum of the air conditioner or people walking by my desk to go to the breakroom. I can just zero in and get the work done. \n\nSo, no, the focus itself isn't the issue at all. Like I was saying earlier, it's more... remembering to start the task, or remembering where I put the physical piece of paper I need for the task. If I have the file right in front of me, boom, I\n\nDoctor: When did this worsening of your forgetfulness begin?\n\nPatient: When did it start getting worse... well, it's actually pretty easy to pinpoint, now that I think about it. It was about 15 months ago. \n\nThat was when I decided to take on this evening certificate program. I've been a secretary for a long time, and I really like my job, but I thought, you know, maybe if I get this business administration certificate, I could eventually move up to an office manager position. It seemed like such a great idea at the time. My husband was super supportive, he was like, \"You'd be great at that, you're so organized at work!\" Which is ironic now, right? \n\nBut almost as soon as I started those classes, everything just started slipping. Because suddenly, I'm working my full shift at the office—which, like I said, I have my whole routine for—but then I have to rush straight over to the community college on Tuesdays and Thursdays, and then I have coursework on the weekends. It's just like my brain hit a wall. \n\nThe sticky notes and the color-coded folders I mentioned earlier? They just aren't enough anymore. I'm trying to juggle the office managers' schedules, my own homework, reading assignments, and just regular life stuff like grocery shopping and laundry, and that's when I really noticed that my attention and my organization were just... tanking. It's been noticeably harder to manage everything since I started that program. I just feel like I'm constantly dropping the ball now, leaving things behind or forgetting what I was supposed to be doing, whereas before I had it all perfectly balanced. So, yeah, it's been about a year and three months of just feeling completely overwhelmed by it all."
  }
]

Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWorrying? Um... no, I don't think so. I mean, not about a lot of different things, anyway. Like, obviously I get frustrated with myself about the things I just mentioned, the dropping the ball at work and forgetting my errands, because that's just so unlike me and it's super annoying. But I wouldn't call it worrying, exactly? \n\nLike, my mom is a huge worrier—she will literally lose sleep over whether she left the coffee maker on or if the neighbor's dog is eating enough, just random stuff all the time. But I've never really been like that. I'm usually the one telling everyone else to just take a breath and calm down. I don't sit around stressing about finances or health or, I don't know, what people think of me. If I forget to return a client's call, I don't agonize over whether they're secretly mad at me or if I'm going to get fired, I just think, 'Ugh, seriously, again?' and I fix it and apologize. \n\nSo it's more just... irritation with my own brain right now, rather than feeling anxious or worried about life in general. I just like things to be handled, and when they aren't, I get annoyed at myself, but I definitely don't spend my day worrying about everything under the sun. I've always been pretty even-keeled that way, which is why this whole forgetting phase is so baffling to me, because I don't even have the excuse of being stressed out about a bunch of other things, you know?\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.
